She tried everything—water therapy, acupuncture, some kind of special glucosamine powder she had to order from New Zealand. It smelled like dead fish. She'd mix it into his kibble and the whole kitchen would just reek. I don't know how the dog ate it. I wouldn't eat anything that smelled like that. It's like those fish oil pills my doctor tried to get me to take for my cholesterol. I burped up salmon flavor for three days before I threw the whole bottle in the trash. I told him I'd rather just eat oatmeal. Oatmeal is supposed to be good for your cholesterol, right? I buy the steel-cut kind, not those little packets full of sugar. Though they do take forever to cook. \n\nBut yeah, I definitely avoid thinking about it or talking about it. I just make an excuse and leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself from afar when those memories come up?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No, I don't think so. I've never felt anything weird like that. I'm definitely always just right here in my own skin. \n\nBut speaking of watching things from afar, we just had to install a whole new security camera system at the warehouse. The regional director decided the old ones were too grainy, which, to be fair, they were. You couldn't tell if a guy on the floor was holding a clipboard or a sandwich. But this new system, it's all high definition, 4K or whatever they call it. I can sit in my office and literally read the shipping labels on the pallets from the ceiling camera. My nephew set up something similar at his house for his front porch. He catches all these people letting their dogs go to the bathroom on his lawn without picking it up. He actually printed out a picture of this one woman—clear as day, you could see her bright pink tracksuit—and taped it to the neighborhood community board. I told him that was a bit aggressive, but people really are just so inconsiderate these days. Like, just yesterday I saw someone leave their shopping cart right in the middle of a parking space instead of walking ten feet to the corral. I mean, how lazy can you be? A sudden gust of wind catches that thing and it's going right into the side of someone's car. It happened to my sister's Honda Civic back in 2018, left a dent the size of a cantaloupe. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't ever feel like I'm floating outside myself or watching myself like a movie or anything. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or disconnected from the world around you when thinking about what happened on that day?\n\nPatient: Numb or disconnected? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel numb. I mean, my hands get a little numb sometimes, but my doctor said that's just mild carpal tunnel from typing too much. \n\nSpeaking of typing, they just switched our inventory software at work, and it is a complete disaster. The old system was perfectly fine. I've been using it for fifteen years. I knew all the keyboard shortcuts, I could enter a whole shipment with my eyes closed. But no, corporate decided we needed something \"cloud-based.\" Whatever that means. So now, every time I try to log a pallet of widgets, I have to click through six different drop-down menus, and half the time the screen just freezes with this little spinning wheel. It's infuriating. I called IT about it, and the guy—he sounded like he was about twelve years old—told me to try clearing my cache. I don't even know what a cache is. I told him I needed a system that actually works, not computer jargon. It's just like when my cable company sent me a new remote control. It had no numbers on it, just a microphone button. You're supposed to talk to it to change the channel. But it never understands me. I say \"HGTV\" and it puts on the History Channel. I ended up having to go down to the Comcast office in person and wait in line for forty-five minutes just to get a regular remote with actual buttons. The lady behind me in line was complaining about her internet bill being too high, and honestly, I had to agree with her. They sneak in all these broadcast fees and regional sports fees... I don't even watch sports! Why should I pay for the local baseball games? \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't feel numb or disconnected from the world. I'm very much plugged into everything going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about what happened on that day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I said before, I try to push it all away. I keep dodging anything that even smells like that old job. Office buildings, those security talks, networking events near the work sites... and if people start asking about the day it happened, I just make some excuse and get out. \n\nI'm actually getting pretty good at making excuses. The other day, I told the mailman I had something burning on the stove just so I wouldn't have to talk to him. He's a nice enough guy, I guess, but he always wants to chat about the weather or his dog's hip dysplasia. I mean, I feel bad for the dog, but what am I supposed to do about it? I'm not a vet. My sister had a golden retriever with bad hips once. She spent thousands of dollars on surgery, and the poor thing still walked with a limp. She tried everything—water therapy, acupuncture, some kind of special glucosamine powder she had to order from New Zealand. It smelled like dead fish. She'd mix it into his kibble and the whole kitchen would just reek. I don't know how the dog ate it. I wouldn't eat anything that smelled like that. It's like those fish oil pills my doctor tried to get me to take for my cholesterol. I burped up salmon flavor for three days before I threw the whole bottle in the trash. I told him I'd rather just eat oatmeal. Oatmeal is supposed to be good for your cholesterol, right? I buy the steel-cut kind, not those little packets full of sugar. Though they do take forever to cook. \n\nBut yeah, I definitely avoid thinking about it or talking about it. I just make an excuse and leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or detached from yourself when thinking about what happened on that day?\n\nPatient: In a daze? No, not really. I mean, I know exactly who I am and where I am. I don't get detached like that. \n\nThe only time I ever really feel like I am in a daze is when I have to sit under those awful new fluorescent lights they just put in the breakroom at work. I swear, they buzz. You can actually hear them humming if the refrigerator isn't running. It is this high-pitched sound that just drills right into your skull. I told the facilities guy about it. His name is Gary, nice enough guy but he moves at an absolute snail's pace. Anyway, I told him they were giving me a headache, and he just said they are energy efficient. Well, great, we are saving three cents a month on the electric bill, but everyone is walking around squinting. \n\nI actually went out to Target on my lunch break yesterday to buy a little desk lamp so I could just turn off the overheads in my office. But you know, they rearrange that store every other week. I went to where the lamps used to be, over by the home goods, and suddenly it is all throw pillows. Just aisles and aisles of pillows. Who needs that many pillows? My ex-husband used to have this thing about putting decorative pillows on the bed, and it drove me absolutely crazy. You just have to take them all off to go to sleep anyway. It is completely pointless. Just a waste of time making the bed in the morning. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't get detached from myself or anything when those thoughts come up. I am completely present.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as issues with relationships or work, because of your avoidance of things that remind you of your old job or that day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has caused some issues. I've actually turned down a few interviews lately, and I stopped taking on my usual volunteer responsibilities. I just can't reliably get through meetings or site visits anymore without shutting down or having to leave early. It's frustrating because I used to be so involved. Even with my family and my mentoring circles, I'm just less present. People have started noticing that I pull back instead of leading like I used to. \n\nSpeaking of leading, I used to run the neighborhood watch committee. We had meetings every first Tuesday of the month at the community center. It was a good group, mostly, except for this one guy, Arthur. Arthur was obsessed with people's trash cans. If you left your bin out past 5:00 PM on collection day, he was knocking on your door with a clipboard, taking notes. He acted like he was the mayor of the cul-de-sac. I finally had to tell him, \"Arthur, we are here to look out for burglaries, not enforce municipal waste ordinances.\" He got so huffy about it. He actually started a rival email chain just to complain about the landscaping crew blowing leaves into the street. Honestly, some people just have too much time on their hands. It's like my sister-in-law, she took up knitting during the pandemic and now everyone gets these lumpy, itchy scarves for Christmas. You can't even wear them, they shed everywhere. I wore one to the grocery store once and by the time I got to the checkout, it looked like I had a small mammal clinging to my coat. The cashier actually asked me if I owned a cat. I don't even like cats. They're too aloof. Give me a dog any day, well, maybe not one with hip dysplasia, but you know what I mean. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question, my avoidance has definitely made it hard to work or be as involved with people as I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened on that day?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't feel like it was my fault or anything. I don't sit around feeling guilty about it. \n\nI do feel guilty about my garden this year, though. I completely neglected my tomatoes. I usually have these big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes by August, but this summer I just couldn't be bothered to go out there and weed. The crabgrass completely took over. It's amazing how fast that stuff grows. You turn your back for two days and it's suddenly a jungle. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she has this pristine yard. Not a blade of grass out of place. She actually hires a service to come spray for weeds every month. But I don't like using chemicals. You never know what's in that stuff, and it gets into the groundwater. I read an article once about how those weed killers are affecting the local frog populations. It's terrible. I used to catch frogs in the creek behind my house when I was a little girl. We'd put them in an old coffee can and watch them jump. You don't see as many frogs these days. I wonder if it's the chemicals or just all the new development they're building down by the highway. They put up a whole new strip mall where that old farm used to be. \n\nBut no, I don't feel guilty about that day. Just about my poor tomatoes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. I mean, salsa music, painting, cooking Sunday dinner... those things used to lift me up, you know? Now I just go through the motions, and that good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to. \n\nSunday dinners used to be a whole production. I'd start prepping on Saturday. I make this incredible pot roast. The secret is searing the meat first and then using a splash of red wine in the gravy. My mother taught me that. She always said you can't rush a good gravy. But you know, finding a good cut of chuck roast lately is nearly impossible. The grocery store down the street, they changed their butcher, and the quality has just gone downhill. Half the time, it's mostly fat, and they charge you an arm and a leg for it. I was in there last week, and I saw the price of eggs—don't even get me started on the eggs. It's highway robbery. I actually thought about getting a few chickens for the backyard, just to have my own eggs. My cousin Edna has chickens. She lives out in the country, though. She has these beautiful Rhode Island Reds. But they are so noisy, and the feed attracts mice. Edna had to get two barn cats just to keep the mice away from the coop. And I already told you how I feel about cats. Plus, with my luck, the neighborhood association would fine me. They have a rule against everything. You can't even paint your front door a color that isn't on their \"pre-approved\" list. I wanted to paint mine a nice, deep navy blue, but no, it had to be \"colonial slate.\" Who even comes up with these names? \n\nBut yeah, the painting, the cooking, the music... it's just not the same anymore. I just don't feel that spark from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person or that the world is a dangerous place because of what happened on that day?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm a bad person, no. But the world being a dangerous place... yes, absolutely. Since then I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust, because even someone you managed or tried to help can turn around and become dangerous. \n\nYou think you know people, especially when you work with them every day and try to guide them. Like this one girl I used to manage, Brenda. I spent so much time trying to help her get her act together. She was sweet enough, she’d bring in these elaborate three-tier cakes for everyone's work anniversary, but she could never manage to file her weekly reports on time. It was always some wild excuse. Once she told me her golden retriever chewed through her Wi-Fi router. I mean, really? But I tried to help her, I really did. I even bought her this beautiful, expensive planner from that little stationery boutique downtown to help her organize her time. \n\nHave you been to that shop? It smells like lavender and old paper when you walk in. I just love it. They have these gorgeous glass dip pens and imported wax seals. I actually bought a wax seal kit for my nephew when he graduated high school last year. A nice brass stamp with his initial and this deep burgundy wax. He didn't even write me a thank-you note for it! Kids these days just send a text with a thumbs-up emoji and think that counts as gratitude. A little cartoon hand on a screen does not replace a proper, handwritten note on heavy cardstock. I always keep a box of embossed cards in my desk drawer. You just never know when you need to send a proper note, and people really do appreciate the effort of a postage stamp. \n\nBut anyway, what were we saying? Oh, right, trusting people. Yeah, it just feels like you can't really trust anyone to be safe anymore, no matter what you do for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel extremely upset or distressed when you're reminded of what happened on that day?\n\nPatient: Upset? No, I wouldn't say I get extremely upset or distressed. I mean, I don't really like thinking about it, but it's not like I fall apart if something reminds me of it. I just try to keep busy, you know? \n\nKeeping busy is the best way to handle most things, honestly. That's why I started reorganizing my pantry last week. I bought all these clear plastic bins with the little chalkboard labels. I spent hours transferring all the flour and sugar and rice into them. It looks like one of those magazines now. But you know, finding a good white chalk marker is surprisingly difficult. The first one I bought was too thick, and it made my handwriting look like a kindergartener's. Then the second one dried out after two labels. I had to drive all the way to the big craft store out by the interstate just to find a decent one. \n\nThe traffic out there is just horrendous lately. They've been doing construction on the overpass for what feels like three years. The other day, I was stuck behind a cement mixer for forty-five minutes. And the driver was smoking, just flicking his ashes right out the window. It's so inconsiderate. I used to smoke, back in my twenties, but I quit cold turkey when they started raising the prices. A pack of cigarettes used to cost next to nothing, and now you need a small loan just to buy one. My uncle Arthur smoked three packs a day his whole life. He had this deep, gravelly voice, like a radio announcer. He used to tell the best stories about his time in the Navy. He was stationed in Hawaii in the fifties. He always said the pineapples there tasted completely different than the ones you get at the grocery store here. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't get extremely distressed or anything like that. I just keep moving forward."
  }
